[trialAccount t18430508-1667]
1667. ROBERT WILSON was indicted for stealing, on 18th of April, 2 breast-pins and chain, value 16 s., the goods of Frederick Shemeld, from his person.
FREDERICK SHEMELD . I am a chaser, and live in Clerkenwell-close. On Tuesday, the 18th of April, I was at a tavern at Greenwich, at the fair time—about half-past ten o'clock in the evening I was passing the bar, to leave the tavern, and saw the prisoner—he said nothing, but came close to my person, and took a union pin out of my scarf—the space before the bar was very crowded, but I can speak positively to the prisoner—I had never seen him before—he passed it to another person—I seized the man he passed it to, collared him, and got him down into a corner of the room—I was immediately set upon by six, eight, or ten persons, and was dreadfully treated—I was not able to attend to my business for some days—the man whom I took hold of got away—I bled very much at the time—I got some hot water—it was perhaps half an hour before I could leave the house—I then left, and in going up High-street, Greenwich, I saw the prisoner—I attempted to seize him, and in passing between two oyster-stalls, or something, I was pushed back by a party, and he made his escape—I afterwards got into a fly, and went into the Elephant and Castle—I felt fatigued, and went into the Alfred's Head, near there, and saw the prisoner there—that must have been past one o'clock in the morning—I pointed him out to the police, and he was taken.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. What time was it you had the pin stolen? A. I should say about half-past ten o'clock—I had gone to the fair about six o'clock—I had not dined there—I had drunk very little—I had been to one other public-house, and about the park—I was not at all intoxicated—I had two or three friends with me, but I was the first who went down stairs—my friend Davis was following me—after I was ill-treated, I went to another house, where the fly stood at the door, and in going to that house I saw the prisoner—I remained in that public-house, I suppose, half an hour—I did not drink—I was much hurt, but not much confused—I did not notice the prisoner's dress so much as his features—I noticed nothing particular about his dress—he had a hat on, I am quite certain—I and my friend did not talk over the description of the person—I did not describe him to my friend, nor he to me—when I met him in High-street, I said, "That is the man who robbed me"—he was passing me at the time, meeting me—he ran away.
JOHN DAVIS . I am a chaser. I was with Shemeld at Greenwich fair—he was coming out of the tavern—I did not see the robbery committed—I saw him directly afterwards—I and my friends were pushed back by a quantity of persons, there might be a dozen, and kept back on the staircase—I came home afterwards with the prosecutor, and was with him when he saw the prisoner in the street—he pointed him out to me—I was with him afterwards at the Alfred's Head, and saw the prisoner there—he is the person I saw at Greenwich.
Cross-examined. Q. The man did not give you much opportunity of examining his countenance? A. No, but I had seen him previously in the house—when I saw him at Greenwich he was under a lamp-post—I saw his countenance well—I have no doubt the man I saw in the street, was the man I had seen before in the public-house—I had noticed him in front of the bar before the occurrence took place—I do not know that I have said so before—I believe I have—when I saw him in the street he made off as soon as he could—neither 1 nor Shemeld had described the person of the man, I am confident, nor talked about it.
WILLIAM DERRY . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner into custody on Wednesday morning, the 19th, at the Alfred's Head—I told him he was charged with robbing a gentleman at Greenwich of a union-pin—he did not say anything, but immediately threw himself on his back, and resisted most Violently.
Cross-examined. Q. How long after you collared him did he throw him-self on his back? A. Almost immediately.
GUILTY . Aged 27.— Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18430508-1668]
1668. MICHAEL MACK was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of April, 1 stock and 3 bits, value 1 l. 11 s.; 3 saws, 19 s.; 2 squares, 6 s.; 4 planes, 1 l.; 1 plough, 1 l.; and 1 gauge, 3 s.; the goods of Edwin Miles.
ESDWINS MILES . I am a carpenter, and live in Marlborough-street, Greenwich. This gimlet and three centre-bits are mine—I saw them safe on Friday night at No. 19, Ship and Billet-row, where I was working—I left them there locked up in a back room—I missed them on Saturday morning at half-past six o'clock, on going to work, and found the lock burst open—the prisoner had been working there a day or two previous—I missed three saws, four planes, and these things—I have never recovered any but these bits and the gimlet—I know these are all mine—I have a particular mark on one—I have been in the habit of using them daily.
CHARLES PHYPERS . I am a carpenter, and live at Royal-hill, Greenwich, On the 15th of April I bought these three centre-bits and gimlet of the prisoner for 10 d.—I asked how he came by them—he said they were given to him by a gentleman, in East-lane, Greenwich, who was going abroad.
Prisoner. I never said so; I said I had found them in the rubbish. Witness. He did not.
EDWARD BUCKLEY . I am a policeman. I apprehended the prisoner—I found nothing in his possession.
Prisoner's Defence. I found the things in the rubbish; I did not think they were of any value, and put them into my pocket.
GSUILTY . Aged 33.— Confined Two Months.
[trialAccount t18430508-1669]
1669. MICHAEL GLEESON was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of April, 1 handkerchief, value 8 d., the goods of George Wigan, from his person.
GEORGE WIGAN . I am a musical instrument maker, and live in Vauxhall-road. On the 17th of April, about half-past four o'clock in the afternoon, I was at Greenwich fair—I felt a tug at my left coat pocket, turned, and saw my handkerchief in the prisoner's hand—it had been in my pocket some minutes before—there was a constable behind me, who took the prisoner—this is my handkerchief.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. You were standing opposite Richardson's booth? A. Yes—I turned round immediately on feeling the tug, and saw the handkerchief, the prisoner, and the constable—I cannot say whether the handkerchief was in my pocket or hanging out.
THOMAS NORRIS ( police-constable A 175.) I was on duty in Greenwich fair on the 17th of April, and saw the prisoner take the prosecutor's pocket in his hand, and feel if there was anything in it—he then put his hand in, and took this handkerchief out—I took him into custody—the crowd was very great—I could not keep hold of him—another constable took him before he got out of my sight—he did not get more than two yards from me.
Cross-examined. Q. You were on the look out for thieves that afternoon? A. Yes—I saw the prisoner attempt to pick several pockets, and others also—I did not see any of them really pick a pocket—another officer,
named Millingham, was in my company—I do not know his number—he belongs to the B division—the Magistrate did not require his evidence—he was before the Magistrate, and gave his evidence, but his deposition was not taken—he is here now—I did not bring him—he has other cases here—I saw the prisoner make two or three attempts to pick pockets—I can swear to three times—I have inquired after his character—I have not found it a very good one—I went to where he had been in service nine months—the policeman, D 146, stated that he had been the constant associate of thieves—I do not know that I ought to have taken the prisoner into custody for attempting to pick a pocket—I have been in the police eight years—if there was nothing in the pocket, he could not take it—I know that attempting to pick a pocket is a misdemeanour—I have had such a case—I know there was nothing in the pockets that he could take, because nothing was taken—I did not consider that he had committed any offence for which I could take him—we sometimes get expenses on summary convictions before a Magistrate—we do not for misdemeanours—we, of course, get expenses here in cases of felony—I have heard that I could have taken him for merely lifting a pocket up, but I did not consider it sufficient for me to take him—I did not run after him—I took him on the spot, as soon as he took the handkerchief out—there was a great crowd—I had been following him about the fair—I first saw him in the crowd, within about a hundred yards of where he stole this handkerchief—there were a great number of persons between me and him, as I followed him through the fair—he escaped from my hand after I seised him, but the other constable seised him, without my losing sight of him—some of the persons in the crowd came between me and him, but he did not get two yards from me—the other constable was on my right side, the side nearest the prisoner, and close to him—as soon as he got out of my hand the officer laid hold of him—the signature to this deposition is mine—( read)—"I took the handkerchief from the prisoner, who escaped in the crowd; I did not lose sight of him, and the prisoner was taken by another constable."
GUILTY . Aged 15.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18430508-1670]
1670. THOMAS HOCKODAY was indicted for stealing, on the 21st of April, 11 penkives, value 16 s. 6 d., the goods of James Cross.
HENRY GLAISER . I am apprentice to Mr. James Cross, a stationer, at Greenwich. On Friday morning, the 21st of April, I came into the shop, and saw the prisoner there with a card of knives in his hand—he put them down, and asked how much they were each—I saw another card of knives under his coat, and asked what he had got there—he said, "Nothing"—I said "You have"—he put the knives down, and ran out of the shop—there was no one else in the shop—I called Mr. Cross, who ran after him—I followed shortly after, overtook Mr. Cross, and spoke to a policeman, who ran after him—he got away in the grove—I am sure he is the person—these are the knives he had under his coat—they are Mr. Cross's.
DANIEL MOYNAHAN ( police-constable R 104.) I was on duty at Croom's-hill, Greenwich, on Friday, the 21st of April. In consequence of information, I pursued the prisoner—I lost sight of him in Blackheath-grove—I only saw his back.
JOSEPH GREEN . I am a policeman. I apprehended the prisoner in the Broadway, Deptford, on the 21st of April, in consequence of information.
Prisoner's Defence. I was returning home from my work, the policeman stopped me, and said one of his men wanted me; the lad at first said he could not swear to me, and next day he said I was the man.
GUILTY . Aged 10.— Confined Three Months.
[trialAccount t18430508-1671]
1671. MARY ANN BROWN was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of April, 1 purse, value 6 d.; 1 knife, 2 d.; 3 half-crowns, 6 shillings, 6 six-pences, and 2 halfpence; the property of Simon Watling, from his person.
SIMON WATLING . I am a Greenwich pensioner. On the 19th of April, about twelve, or half-past twelve o'clock in the day, I met the prisoner in the street, between Deptford and Greenwich—I never knew her before—I do not know whether she spoke to me, or I to her, hut we went and drank together outside a public-house, sitting on the bench—before I met her I had a purse with three half-crowns, three shillings, six sixpences, two halfpence, a knife, and an old ring in it—it belonged to me—I lost my purse—I cannot say whether I dropped it on the ground, or how it was, but it was found in the prisoner's lap—I did not feel anything happen to my person while she was with me—this is the purse, knife, and ring, now produced—I was not sober.
MARY ANN PRESTON . I was sitting at the beer-shop, taking refreshment, and saw the prisoner and prosecutor drinking together—I saw him take the purse from his pocket, shake it on the stool, and say that money was to take him home—he put it into his pocket again—about five minutes after, I saw the prisoner get up, and take the purse from his pocket—this is the purse.
Prisoner. Q. Did you not hear me say to the prosecutor, "There is your purse," and see me deliver it into his hands? A. You did not give up the purse till you were accused—you said he was your husband, and you had a right to the purse.
WILLIAM TURNER . I am a policeman. I took the prisoner into custody—she said the prosecutor was her husband.
Prisoner. Q. Did not the prosecutor throw the money on the table, and did not you take it up? A. The money was spread on the table—you had no money.
"The prisoner put in a written defence, stating that the prosecutor pulled out his purse several times; that she persuaded him to keep it in his pocket; that on his putting it in he left one end hanging out; that she took hold of it and placed it in his hand, and he scattered the contents on the table, and that the females fetched a policeman, and gave her in charge."
GUILTY. Aged 44.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Two Months.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18430508-1672]
1672. GEORGE GRAY was indicted for stealing, on the 15th of Aug., at Lee, 1 cash-box, value 6 d.; 16 spoons, 3 l. 8 s.; 1 pair of sugar-tongs, 8 s.; 1 sovereign, 1 half-sovereign, 9 half-crowns, 106 shillings, 77 sixpences, 28 groats, 1 half-crown, 1 sixpence, 1 groat, and 1 foreign silver coin, value 1 s., the property of Henry Couldery, in his dwelling-house.
MR. BALLANTINE conducted the Prosecution.
HENRY COULDERY . I keep the Royal Oak, in the parish of Lee. On Monday, the 15th of August, about two o'clock in the afternoon, the prisoner and another person, came to my house, and directly ran up into the club-room—there is a passage between the club-room and my bed-room—the doors are opposite each other—I kept a cash-box in my bedroom—the prisoner called for two slices of bread and cheese, and a pint of stout, and afterwards for another pint of stout, and a screw of tobacco—when I went up the third time, the prisoner said, "Landlord, will you let the door be open, for your room is so excessively hot"—I opened the club-room door at his request, and went down again—I observed a pocket-book on the table—about half an hour afterwards I saw the prisoner and the other coming down stairs—(Mrs.
Couldery had then been up into the bed-room)—they went out without paying for their refreshment—I followed them as far at the threshold of the door—I then waited to know if my bed-room was safe—after some communication from my wife, I followed the prisoner—I overtook him about 100 yards from my house, and took him into custody—I do not know that he saw me before I came up to him—he could have seen me, because the ground rises—the other man went across the fields towards the Lee-road—I have since seen my cash-box—at the time it was stolen, it contained gold, half-crowns, shillings, and sixpences—it was in a chest of drawers in my bed-room.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. How far is the club-room from the bed-room? A. The passage runs between the two—there had been three other persons drinking in the club-room besides these two—I had never seen the prisoner before—I have two servants.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. When the prisoner and the other first came, were there any persons up in the club-room? A. There was—they did not remain there all the while—two of them came down about a minute before these two—they paid for what they had, and went away—the other one came down with these two—he was a deaf and dumb gentleman, living in the neighbourhood, who used to come there regularly every afternoon—the door of the club-room is in the centre of the room—the prisoner might have closed the door so that the persons in the club-room could not see him go into the bed-room—I did not know either of the other two persons—they had been in the house before, and up into the club-room—I have never seen them since—I did not observe the prisoner in conversation with any of the other persons in the club-room—they seemed to be keeping separate company.
AMELIA COULDERY . I am the prosecutor's wife. On the 15th of Aug. I went up into the bed-room by my husband's direction—as I went up I met some persons coming down stairs—the prisoner was one of them—I am quite sure of him—I went into my bed-room, and found the drawer had been broken open, and the cash-box gone—I had seen it half an hour before—it then had gold and silver in it—I called out to my husband—there was no marks of the drawer having been forced—I saw the two persons come in before the prisoner and the other man, soon after two o'clock—the prisoner came not long after with the other man—I was up in the room at the time the prisoner was there, and saw him in the room—the club-room door is usually kept open.
WILLIAM ARCHER . I am a gardener, and live at Lee. I was at Mr. Couldery's house on the 15th of Aug., and saw the prisoner—I was in the club-room several times—I was sitting at the bottom of the stairs at the time the prisoner and the other man came down stairs, and left the house—I followed the prisoner with Mr. Couldery—we took him—I told him I took him on suspicion of robbing Mr. Couldery of a cash-box—he denied it.
CHARLES WRIGHT . I am a carpenter, living at Lee. On the 16th of Aug. I was going from Church-street, through Church-passage, to Lee, and under a hedge there I found this cash-box and a parcel near it, in which I found some plate—I had heard of the robbery the night before, and took it to the prosecutor—Mr. Couldery has since pointed out to me the spot where the prisoner was taken, and it was about eighty yards beyond that that the cash-box was found—I showed Mr. Couldery where I found it.
HENRY COULDERY re-examined. Wright showed me where he found the cash-box—the prisoner took that course on leaving my house, and would pass that spot—I took the prisoner against a large tree about eighty yards from there.
JAMES HIGGS ( police-constable R 233.) I searched the prisoner and found
this piece of iron-wire, and pocket-book on him—I produce the lock of the drawer which was broken open—there is one mark on it which distinctly corresponds with the point of this iron—I also saw some marks on the drawer. John Wyatt. I am a smith, and live at Lee. This iron is a bottle-jack skewer—I examined the lock of the drawer, and found the bolt had been forced down by some sharp-pointed instrument—this iron would have done it—I also saw marks on the wood work of the drawer, which I have no doubt were made by that instrument.
Cross-examined. A. Any sharp thing would produce the same effect? A. It might—I have none of the wood work here—there is an impression on the bolt of the lock.
MR. BALLANTINE. Q. The wood work is on a chest of drawers, is it not? A. Yes, and on the drawer itself—the marks correspond in size and shape with this instrument.
JOHN CARPENTER ( police-sergeant R 84.) I searched the prisoner's lodging the night after he was apprehended—he told me previously where he lived—I found a quantity of keys, a dark-lantern, and other articles there, which I produce—I took Wyatt to the prosecutor's house by the Magistrate's order—I found marks on the drawer corresponding with the top of this skewer—it is rather a peculiar shape—it exactly fits the marks in the wood work of the drawer—there were two marks—I have no doubt they were made by this—the marks were about a quarter of an inch apart, both on one side, directly opposite the bolt of the lock.
FRANCIS BARNARD ( police-constable R 134.) I had charge of the prisoner, to take him from Maidstone to Greenwich, on board a steam-packet—when the packet stopped at Cray, in Essex, I found he had escaped—he had been below—I went to his house, but could not find him.
ARTHUR PASCOE ( police-constable H 141.) In consequence of information, I took the prisoner into custody on Friday, the 14th of April, near the Liverpool-road—a person was with me who pointed him out to me—the prisoner saw the person put up his finger to me, and directly started off as hard as he could go—I ran after him a considerable distance, and succeeded at last in taking him—I asked if his name was Gray—he said it was not—I said I thought it was—he said it was not—I took him by the collar, and took him to the station—as I was afterwards taking him from the station to a cab, he said I was the only one who could do it for him—I understood what he meant, but made little or no reply—after we were in the cab he kept talking to me in the same strain respecting money—I did not answer—he asked me if I heard what he said—I said I did—he said, "I am afraid you don't heed; if you don't, I am cooked; I don't think you understand business."
Cross-examined. Q. Was any one present at this conversation but you and him? A. No—the party who identified him was sitting on the coach-box.
HENRY COULDERY re-examined. The pocket-book I saw on the table in the club-room was similar to the one produced—it was a red morocco one—this plate and cash-box is mine, and what I missed from the drawer—the prisoner was about sixty yards from my house when I began to run after him—the other man did not run in that direction—the prisoner was the only one that took that direction—he might have thrown away the cash-box without my seeing him do so—he had to go under two arches, which would obstruct the sight.
GUILTY . * Aged 30.— Transported for Ten Years.
Before Mr. Baron Gurney.
[trialAccount t18430508-1673]
1673. THOMAS SMITH and EDWARD READING were indicted for feloniously and knowingly uttering a counterfeit half-crown to Margaret Butler, on the 18th of April; they having been previously convicted of uttering counterfeit coin.
MESSRS. BODKIN and ESPINASSE conducted the Prosecution.
CALEB EDWARD POWELL . I am assistant solicitor to the Mint. I produce a copy of the record of the conviction of Thomas Smith at the Hertford sessions in Jan., 1842—I also produce a copy of the record of the conviction of Edward Reading at this Court at the Jan. sessions, 1838—I have examined both with the original records, and they are true copies—( read.)
WILLIAM FARRELL . I am a schoolmaster, and live at Wynford, in Hert-fordshire. In 1842 I was in the Rural Police—I know the prisoner Smith—I was present in Court, at Hertford, when he was tried for uttering a counterfeit shilling—he was convicted, and came out of gaol in Jan. last year.
JAMES BROOK ( police-constable L 118.) In Jan., 1838, I was present in this Court when Reading was tried and convicted of uttering counterfeit coin.
JAMES FLEMING . I am in the general line, and live at Greenwich. On the evening of the 18th of April I was at my stall, between seven and eight o'clock—the prisoner Smith came and asked for a penny polony and a penny cake, and gave me a shilling—I gave it to my wife, who was with me—she gave him change, and he went away.
Smith. Q. What sort of a shilling was it, a new or old one? A. It was not a new one exactly; it was a dirty looking one.
SARAH ANN FLEMING . I am the wife of James Fleming. On the evening of the 18th of April he sold a cake and a polony to a man, and gave me the shilling he received in payment—I gave change for it, and put it into my pocket—I had some sixpences there, but no other shilling, I am sure—about an hour after I went to give change again, and found it was a bad one—I had no other shilling—I gave it to the officer next morning—I am sure it was the same shilling I had received from my husband.
Smith. Q. Who did you give the change to? A. I did not take particular notice who it was.
RICHARD FOLKES ( police-constable R 203.) I was on duty in the neighbourhood of Greenwich on Tuesday evening, the 18th of April, about six o'clock—I saw the prisoners in Stockwell-street, Greenwich, in company with a female, loitering about, from half to three-quarters of an hour—it was fair time—I saw them go towards Straits-mouth, which is near Fleming's stall—I saw no more of them that day—next morning Fleming came to me, told me something, and gave me a shilling, which I produce—I believe he marked it before he gave it me.
MARGARET BUTLER . I keep the Painter's Arms public-house, Strait's-mouth, Greenwich. On the evening of the 18th of April the prisoners came to my house together—I served Smith with a pint of ale—Reading was with him at the time—Smith gave me a half-crown in payment—I immediately observed that it was a bad one—I marked it, and put it on the shelf-I told Smith it was a bad one, and I should not return it to him—he said nothing, but immediately gave me a good one—both of the prisoners partook of the ale—Reading said he did not think I was any good myself—after they drank the ale they left the house—I sent Wilson, my waiter, after them—I afterwards gave the half-crown to the policeman.
Smith. I was not aware it was bad; I did not wish to have it returned, but to have it put into the fire. Witness. He did not ask me to return it, or to put it into the fire—he did not say a word about it.
COURT. Q. Did either of them say anything after you refused to return the half-crown? A. Reading used many insulting words, but I cannot say what they were, for I went immediately to look after my waiter, and when I came down they were gone.
JAMES WILSON . I am waiter to Mrs. Butler. On the 18th of April, in consequence of directions from my mistress, I went after the prisoners—they walked away from our house—I saw them at the Earl Grey, at the corner of Skelton's-lane, about a hundred yards from my mistress's house—they were going into the house—they saw me following them, and turned round, and Smith said, "Are you following me, Sir?"—I said, "Never mind, you must mind what you are about"—the police came up, and I gave them into custody—they were both in the attitude of fighting me when I turned round.
Smith. He said at Greenwich that it was Reading that spoke to him. Witness. It was Smith.
SAMUEL ARCHER ( police-constable R 54.) I was on duty near the Earl Grey beer-house on the night of the 18th of April, and received the prisoners in custody from Wilson—I took them to Mrs. Butler's, and received from her this half-crown, which I produce—I found 2 s. 5 d. on Smith, good money, and 4 d. on Reading—Reading was taken sick in the station—we took him out into the air, and he spat 3 s. 6 d. in good money out of his mouth into the yard—he appeared to bring it up from his stomach—Smith said that his character was well known, that he was not a character anybody knew any harm about.
MR. JOHN FIELD . I am inspector of counterfeit coin to the Mint. I have examined this half-crown and shilling—they are both counterfeit in all respects.
Smith's Defence. Fleming states that he cannot tell whether it was an old or a new shilling; he gave it to his wife; she said at first she had it four hours, and now she says it was hardly an hour before she had occasion to go to her pocket to give change; she cannot say it was me gave the half-crown, and it is very strange that she should not know who it was, and he should; the truth of it is the policeman made a search about for some bad money; he found this half-crown, and has persuaded Fleming to say it was me that gave it him; I know nothing about it; I was very tipsy when I went to Mrs. Butler's; I did not wish her to return the half-crown, but gave her another; I have worked hard for my living since I have been out of prison.
Reading's Defence. I have been at work for these eighteen months; it was fair time, and I was very tipsy.
SMITH — GUILTY . Aged 23.
REDDING — GUILTY . Aged 24.
Transported for Ten years.
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18430508-1674]
1674. JOHN MASON was indicted for feloniously assaulting Ellen Mason, on the 6th of May, and cutting and wounding her in and upon the left side of the head, with intent to do her some grievous bodily harm.
ELLEN MASON . I am the prisoner's wife, and live in Prince's-fields, Deptford. On Saturday night, the 6th of May, my husband came home, between ten and eleven o'clock, drunk—we had a few words respecting his relations—I rather provoked him, and was quite as much in fault as he was—I went towards the door—he followed me into the yard, and hit me in the face with his hand or fist, I am not sure which—I believe I took a piece of wood off the paling, and attempted to throw it at him, and he struck me twice with it on the side of my head—it did not knock me down—I was down at the time—I was not knocked down, I fell—I think I must have kicked my foot against a stone—my head felt very painful, and bled a great deal—I thought
something stuck in my head at the time—it was a bit of a nail—this is the stick—I have not been ill at all, or confined.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. I suppose all the injury done was from this nail? A. Yes—this is the piece of wood which I myself tore off, as my boy informed me—I was going to throw it at him when he took it from me—he had not got it in his hand when he came in—after he hit me in the face I struck him on the top of his hat—that was before he struck me with this.
MARY ANDREWS . I am the wife of William Andrews, a pensioner at Deptford—I heard them quarrelling on this evening—I saw the prisoner striking his wife twice with this stick—he was going to strike a third blow, which I prevented—I was there at the beginning—he got the stick off my gate, and struck her on the head.
EDWARD DOWLING . I am a surgeon, and live at Deptford—I examined the prosecutrix, and found a slight wound on her head, as if made by a nail.
GUILTY of an Assault. Aged 52.
To enter into his own recognizances of 50 l. to keep the peace for two years.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.

DANIEL RIORDEN . I am a police-sergeant. In consequence of information, I went after the prisoners, and saw them in High-street, running both together—I took Jones—Hillier was stopped by two soldiers—I found this knife next morning over a wall, in the direction in which they had run.
Jones. We ran, because it rained very hard.

1677. WILLIAM JONES and CHARLES PHILLIPS were indicted for stealing, on the 18th of April, 1 handkerchief, value 2 d., the goods of a man unknown, from his person.
JAMES M'LOCHLAN ( police-constable C 125.) I was in Greenwich-park, on the 18th of April, between five and six o'clock in the evening—I saw the prisoners there together—I saw Phillips take this handkerchief from gentleman's pocket, and pass it to Jones—I do not know who the gentleman was—he was in the crowd—I took Jones, and found this handkerchief in his pocket—Phillips escaped.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did Jones claim the handkerchief? A. No—we could not get Phillips, as the mob was so great—the gentleman was standing looking at a party playingat kiss-in-the-ring—I was in plain clothes.
Phillips. You let us get away from the crowd. Witness. No, I took Jones directly.
JAMES WILLIAM BURRIDGE ( police-constable V 160.) I was in the Park
in plain clothes—I saw Phillips take this handkerchief from a gentleman, and hand it to Jones—I pursued Phillips, but he got out of the Park—I found Phillips in Gravel-lane, in the Borough.
Cross-examined. Q. Who was the gentleman this handkerchief belonged to? A. That I cannot say.
(Jones received a good character.)

Before Mr. Common Sergeant.

1680. CHARLES NICHOLSON and CHARLES ANSLOW were in-dicted for stealing, on the 18th of April, 1 handkerchief, value 6 d.; the goods of John Foster Hillman, from his person; to which
NICHOLSON pleaded GUILTY . Aged 17.— Confined Fourteen Days.
JOHN FOSTER HILLMAN . I am clerk to Mr. Hillman, a merchant in the City. On the 18th of April, between nine and ten o'clock at night, I was in Greenwich fair—I heard some one call—I turned, and saw my handkerchief in the constable's hand—it was safe about a minute before—this is it.
HENRY FOWLER ( police-constable E 111.) I was at Greenwich fair, and saw the prisoners following the prosecutor, and talking together in an under tone—I saw Nicholson take this handkerchief out of the prosecutor's pocket, and give it to Anslow, who had been covering Nicholson, and was near enough to see what he did—I took him, and took the handkerchief from him.
Anslow. I had it not in my possession at all. Witness. I took it from him.

[trialAccount t18430508-1681]
1681. CHARLES COLEMAN was indicted for stealing, on the 19th of April, 2 breast-pins and chain, value 5 l.; the goods of Thomas John Watkins, from his person.
MR. PAYNE conducted the Prosecution.
THOMAS JOHN WATKINS . I am a wine-merchant. On the night of the 19th of April I was at Greenwich fair—I went to the Mitre tavern with a female—I had a gold pin in my scarf, and missed it after I had been there a
short time—this is it—( looking at it)—I had this in my scarf when I went into the Mitre—it is two pins and a chain.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. You had been drinking more wine than you ought to have done? A. I regret to say that was the case—I had not known the female till that evening—I do not consider that I was in a condition to say whether she was sober or not—I was on some business, and met with two friends—I dined with them, and that induced me to go to the fair.
ELLEN KING . I live in Sheffield-street, Lincoln's-inn-fields—I am assistant to a dress-maker—I was at Greenwich fair on the night of the 19th of April with a female companion—I missed her, and while I was looking for her I met with Mr. Watkins—we then went to the Mitre tavern—there was a great crowd—it was just as the fair was over—we were just against the coffee-room door, when the prisoner put his head in my face, and at the same time he drew the pin out of the gentleman's scarf—I caught hold of him by his handkerchief, and called for a police-constable—I kept the prisoner, with the assistance of the crowd, till the police-constable came—I afterwards saw the pin picked off the floor—it was given to me—I handed it over to the person at the bar—I believe this to be it.
Cross-examined. Q. Where does the dress-maker live to whom you are assistant? A. At No. 10, Sheffield-street—I reside in the house, and work for her in my own room—there is a shop there—this happened between eleven and twelve o'clock at night—I had missed my companion for about half an hour—I had not joined company with the prosecutor ten minutes when this happened—there was a great crowd—I had not been taking anything to drink myself—I saw the pin picked up, but I cannot say whether a man or a woman picked it up—I asked for it, and it was handed to me, but I really cannot tell whether it was by a man or a woman—I did not see my friend in the crowd—there were others there that I knew, men and women; but no one that I went with—I had seen them in the fair before I went to the Mitre—I was perfectly sober—I went down with a friend—the prosecutor joined me—I was alone at that time—I had never seen him before.
MARGARET ROBERTS . I am cook at the Mitre, at the corner, near the church, at Greenwich. Between eleven and twelve o'clock on the night of the 19th of April, a pin was handed to me by a female—I wrapped it in paper, and put it on a shelf, till I gave it to the policeman.
Cross-examined. Q. Was it given you by the last witness? A. It might be her, or it might not—I believe she was the woman—I was asked at the Magistrate's if she was tipsy, and I said, "She might be, or might not"—there was a great crowd, I could not tell.
JOSEPH BUCKMASTER ( police-constable R 42.) I was on duty about twelve o'clock at night—I was called into the Mitre—I saw Mr. Watkins, the prisoner, and King—I spoke to King; she appeared to me to be perfectly sober—the prosecutor was rather intoxicated—I received this pin from a female at the bar—I cannot say whether it was Roberts.
GUILTY . Aged 34.— Transported for Ten Years.

Goodman. I know nothing of this lad. I did not not put my hand into any pockets.

1683. HENRY WELLS and EDWARD BROWN were indicted for stealing, on the 17th of April, 1 handkerchief, value 1 s., the goods of John Higgs, from his person; and that Brown had been before convicted of felony; to which

Wells. It was not in my hand; I am quite innocent; I was four yards from Brown. Witness. It was in Wells's hand—he was close by Brown.
GEORGE KEMP ( police-constable N 82.) THOMAS BURCHAM ( police-constable H 33.) I was with Kemp—I saw Wells put bis hand into the prosecutor's pocket, and take out the handkerchief—both the prisoners had hold of it—I took Brown—I found on him three handkerchiefs, one round bis body, one round his neck, and one he threw down.

[trialAccount t18430508-1684]
1684. JOSEPH WATKINS and JOHN NEAVES were indicted for stealing, on the 17th of April, 1 handkerchief, value 2 s., the goods of a man unknown, from his person.
GEORGE KEMP ( police-constable N 82.) I was on duty in Greenwich fair on the 17th of April—I saw the prisoners there—I watched them for some time, and saw Neaves put his hand into a gentleman's pocket, take a handkerchief
out, and pass it to Watkins, who put it into the flap of his trowsers—I and my brother officer took the prisoners into custody, but the crowd being great, we lost the gentleman—in going to the station Watkins took the handkerchief out of his trowsers, and chucked it down.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Where were you standing? A. Close at their back—I could not touch the gentleman, for directly they got the handkerchief they made a push, and I pushed after them—I distinctly saw Neaves take the handkerchief—they were playing in front of a show, and the people were standing close, looking at the clown.
THOMAS BURCHAM ( police-constable H 33.) I was with Kemp—I saw Neaves take this handkerchief from a gentleman's pocket, and pass it to Watkins, who put it into his trowsers' pocket—it came out of his trowsers on the way to the station.
Watkins's Defence. I saw this handkerchief lying down, with some bread and meat in it; I saw some men, and asked them if it was theirs; they said no; I and my sister eat the bread and meat; I put the handkerchief into my pocket; I was looking at Richardson's show, and took the handkerchief oat to wipe my nose, and the officer seised me.
(Neave received a good character.)

NEAVES — GUILTY . Aged 20.— Confined Six Months.

[trialAccount t18430508-1685]
1685. GEORGE SHAW was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of April, 1 cigar-case, value 2 s.; and 4 cigars, 1 s.; the goods of a certain man unknown, from his person.
WSILLIAMS GUEST ( police-constable L 62.) I was at Greenwich fair on the 17th of April, and saw the prisoner following two-gentlemen—he thrust his hand into the coat pocket of one of them, and for a moment I lost sight of him—Brooks caught hold of him, and I saw this cigar-case partly in the prisoner's hand, and partly in the officer's.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Is this the only case you have bad this session from Greenwich fair? A. Yes, and the first case I have had where the property has been taken from, a person unknown—I lost sight of the prisoner for about a second in the crowd—I spoke to both gentlemen, and they came with me to a coffee shop.
COURT. Q. Did you request the gentleman to go to the station? A. Yes, he said he would at first, but he was advised by his friend not to do so, as it would take some time—we requested him to give his name, and he refused to do it.
JAMES BROOK ( police-constable L 118.) I was with Guest—I saw the prisoner put his hand into a gentleman's pocket, and take out this cigar-case—I put my arms in front of him, took it from him, and the lid of it fell on the ground.
Cross-examined. Q. Was there a crowd? A. Yes, a large crowd—I did not lose sight of the prisoner for a moment—I was close by Guest, and I drew his attention to it.
COURT. Q. How came the gentleman not to go to the station? A. It is such a great distance from the fair.
GUILTY . Aged 21.— Confined Six Months.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.

[trialAccount t18430508-1687]
1687. RICHARD WELHAM and WILLIAM CONNOR were indicted for uttering counterfeit coin.
MESSRS. BODKIN and ESPINASSE conducted the Prosecution.
JAMES HOWORTH . I am in the Royal Marines, at Woolwich, and am employed in the barber's shop there. On the 31st of March, Welham came to be shaved—I asked him for the money which he owed me—he said he had not got any, only sham money—he produced two half-sovereigns to me out of his purse, where I saw he had ten or a dozen more—he gave me two, and told me to keep them till he came again; not to part with them, or he would never pay me what he owed me—he was immediately called away by the corporal, and I put the half-sovereigns in a piece of paper into my pocket, and when Suttle came I gave them to him.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Where did you put them? A. In a cupboard, under lock and key—they remained there some days—the policeman came about three weeks alter—my mate has got a key to the place as well as me.
WILLIAM INGLISH . I am a private in the Royal Marines—I have a key to this cupboard—I did not take any half-sovereigns out—I never saw them.
WILLIAM SUTTLE ( police-constable R 260.) I received these two half-sovereigns from Howorth.
ANN SONNEX . I am the wife of John Sonnex, who keeps the Duke on Horseback, in High-street, Woolwich. On the 11th of April I saw my husband clear the till of all the gold coin—there was none left in the till—Connor was employed in waiting in the house as an extra hand during the fair. Between four and five o'clock in the evening of the 13th of April, Welham came in—he asked for half a pint of rum, which came to 1 s.—I put it on the counter—Connor ordered it—Welham came up, and said to Connor, "Bill, is this for me? "Connor said, "Yes, Dick "—Welham said, "Here, take this half-sovereign, "and Connor took it from him, and gave it to me—I gave 9 s. in change to Connor, and he gave it to Welham—I put the half-sovereign into a bowl in the till—there was no other half-sovereign there—when we closed for the night, I took the coin out of the bowl and put it into a bag—there was no other half-sovereign but that, and I took it up stairs—on the 14th of April Welham came again in the evening—it was his general practice to come of an evening—he was in and out a good deal that day, and treating persons with liquor—he asked me for half-a-pint of gin-and-raspberry, which came to 8 d.—I served him—he gave me a half-sovereign, and I gave him 9 s. 4 d. change—Connor stood by, but bad nothing to do with the change—in the course of that night Connor came for a pot of beer, and gave me a half-sovereign—he said it was for Dick, the marine—I gave him the change, and saw him give it to Welham in the passage—Welham said, "It is all right, Bill, you shall never want a shilling while I have one"—on Saturday, the 15th, Welham came again—in the course of that evening I took two half-sovereigns from Connor—he said they were from Dick, the marine—on the 16th Welham came again with a young woman—he gave me a half-sovereign, and I gave him 9 s. 6 d. change—Welham and Connor were always together—that evening Welham proposed to leave a half-sovereign with Connor, wrapped up in paper—Connor said, "Leave it at the bar, I will have nothing to do with
your money"—Welham said, "Give me my money, I am quite capable of taking care of my own money"—I gave it him back—I said, "Richard, your money is all half-sovereigns"—he said, "Yes, my sister, who keeps the Cricketers, lets me have half-a-sovereign a-day, or sometimes two, according as I can get her in a good humour"—on the Monday I received a half-sovereign from Connor—I said it had two or three black specks on the head of it—he said he did not know anything about it, that it belonged to the marine, and that he changed all the half-sovereigns—on Tuesday I received two half-sovereigns from Connor—I saw him give the change to Welham—in the evening of that day I received a half-sovereign from Welham, and he asked me to let Connor go with him to Greenwich fair—I refused, as my husband was not at home—Connor afterwards asked my husband's leave to go—he said he was no servant of bis, be might go where he pleased—he went, and I saw no more of him till the next day—during the whole of this time I did not receive a half-sovereign from any other person—they were all placed in a bowl, and put into the bag at night, and put into the desk, and carried down in the morning—on the 20th of April I looked to my money—I was going to count it—I saw the thirteen half-sovereigns, and it struck me they were different to the sovereigns, and among them was the one with black specks—my husband weighed them, and examined them, and found them bad—I then communicated with the police—Palmer came, and the half-sovereigns were all marked in his presence, and given to him—these now produced are them.
Cross-examined. Q. When was it you received the last? A. On Easter Tuesday—I only serve in the evening—my husband does in the day—we never allow our servants to serve—my husband puts money into the bowl as well as I—there were no half-sovereigns put into the bowl but what I put in—it was Connor's duty to hand the half-sovereigas from Welham to me, and the change to Welham.
JOHN SONNEX . I am husband to Ann Sonnex.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you receive any half-sovereigns from this Thursday to the Tuesday? A. None—I serve in the morning—I did not change a half-sovereign for anybody—I had not the key of the desk—my wife serves in the evening, and day time too—I have other business to attend to—I do hot serve above an hour every day.
EDWARD PALMER ( police-constable R 231.) On the 20th of April I went to Mr. Sonnex—some half-sovereigns were produced—Mrs. Sonnex marked them, and they were given to me—these are them—I took Connor—he said, he knew nothing about it, he took the money from Dick, the marine—I afterwards apprehended Welham—I told him it was for passing bad money—I put him into a coach, and when we were half-way, he said, "Have you apprehended me for passing bad half-sovereigns? "—I said, "Yes, "and he immediately went into a fit.
GEORGE HORSLEY . I keep the Cricketers—my wife is no relation of Welham's—she could not, as his sister, have given him half-sovereigns occasionally.
MR. JOHN FIELD . I am inspector of coin to the Mint—I have examined all these coins—they are all counterfeit half-sovereigns—they have all been struck from one die—the two black marks on this one arise from the gilt having been worn off, and the base metal appearing underneath—the general appearance of them is tolerably good—they are made of brass—they will not ring.
WELHAM — GUILTY . Aged 24.— Confined Two Years.
CONNOR — GUILTY . Aged 20.— Confined Six Months.
SURREY CASES.
Before Mr. Recorder.

1688. MARTHA ROGERS was indicted for stealing, on the 20th of April, 1 sixpence, and 9 groats; the monies of William Hollands, her master.
WILLIAM HOLLANDS . I am clerk to Jones, Loyd, and Co., and live it Beresford-street, Newington—the prisoner was eight days in my service—in consequence of some suspicion, I marked some fourpenny pieces, and other silver, put it into my wife's purse, which I put into her reticule, and left it on the parlour table, as we were in the habit of doing—I locked the parlour door and the key was put on the prisoner's mantel-piece for her to get the room ready in the morning—next morning I found the reticule lying on the table as usual, but missed some fourpenny pieces and a 6 d.—I sent for a constable, and gave the prisoner in charge.
JOHN HUNTLEY ( police-constable P 143.) I took the prisoner in custody, and took her to the station.
SARAH EVEREST . I am the wife of David Everest, a policeman, and live at the station—I searched the prisoner on the 20th of April, and found on her these nine fourpenny-pieces, a fivepenny-piece, another small piece, and a sixpence.
MR. HOLLANDS re-examined. These are what I marked, and put into the purse.
Prisoner. I had some clothes in pawn, which I was very much in want of, and seeing the bag lying on the table three or four days, I was tempted to take the money, which I should not have done, if it had not been lying there.
GUILTY. Aged 17.— Recommended to mercy by the prosecutor.— Judgment Respited.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
[trialAccount t18430508-1689]
1689. WILLIAM GOY was indicted for stealing, on the 17th of Dec., 2 pewter pots, value 2 s.; the goods of Thomas Roberts.
CHARLES MILLER . I live in St. Ann's-street, Kennington—the prisoner was in my employ, as a chimney-sweeper, about seven months—in consequence of something I heard, I said, "William, where are those pots? "—he went to the soot yard, and brought three or four, such as publicans use—I asked how he came by them—he said they came from No. 4, Bedford-terrace—I asked what he was going to do with them—he said, "I do not know;" and then said, smilingly, "To sell them"—I said, "Are you aware what you are about to do? you are liable to be sent to Brixton; do you know who they belong to? "—he said "No"—I said, "You can read what is on them"—I could read it—one part over the inscription had been cleaned—on the 27th of April, a lad in my employ, named Lovejoy, brought me two pint pots—I gave information to the police that afternoon, and gave him the two pots—the others could not be found—I had asked the prisoner if he had taken the pots back—he said he had—I asked what the people said—he said, "Nothing"—I asked what they gave him—he said, "Only something to drink."
WILLIAM LOVE JOY . I live with Mr. Miller—I saw two pewter pots on the window-ledge of the soot-shed, about four months ago—I heard master tell the prisoner to take them home—he did not say where he was to take them—I saw them that same afternoon wrapped up in a handkerchief—last Thursday week, in cleaning out the loft, I found the same two pots under the tiles—I took them to my roaster—these are the pots.
THOMAS ROBERTS . I keep the Bedford Arms public-house, Clapham Rise—these two pots are mine—they were sent out to a customer—I cannot say
when they were at my house—I never saw the prisoner till he was at the office.
JOHN MENNIE . I am a policeman. On Thursday last I was sent for to Mr. Miller's house, and received these two pots.
WILLIAM ERMED . I am a policeman. I apprehended the prisoner—as I took him to the station, he said to a person that walked by his side, that he went to sweep a chimney on the terrace, and, in potting the soot into the bag, a pint pot fell out of his shovel, and he had picked up one previously; that the servant took it up and put it into his bag—he said he did not take it back, as his master told him, in consequence of his old mistress telling him not to do it.
Prisoner's Defence. I went to No. 4, Bedford-terrace, to sweep the kitchen chimneys. The stove was loose, and behind it I found this pot, which is burnt at the bottom, a coffee-pot, and a lot of old saucepan lids. I put them on one side; and as I was putting the soot into the sack, this other pot fell off the shovel; the servant picked it up, and put it into my sack. I did not see them till the soot was sifted, after I got home. I showed them to my master, who told me to take them home; but my mistress told me not to do so, but to leave them there, or throw them away. I left them in the top room. I left my master about six weeks ago, and set up for myself, and have got a few of his customers from him, and this is his spite. He knew the pots were in the house.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430508-1690]
1690. JOHN HARLEY was indicted for breaking and entering the dwelling-house of James Beswick, on the 24th of Dec., and stealing therein, 1 coat, value 1 l. 10 s.; 4 pairs of trowsers, 3 l. 5 s.; 4 waistcoats, 2 l.; 1 pair of breeches, 15 s.; 1 cloak, 1 l.; 1 jacket, 5 s.; 5 handkerchiefs, 12 s.; 1 shawl, 15 s.; 2 gowns, 15 s.; and 1 petticoat, 2 s.; his property.
JAMES BESWICK . I am a lead merchant, and live at No. 97, St. George's-row, Southwark. On Saturday, the 24th of Dec, I left my house, about a qaarter before nine o'clock, leaving no one in it—I double-locked the street-door, and fastened the windows all perfectly safe—I returned about a quarter past nine, with my wife—I found the door on the single lock, but still shut and fastened—I went in, and found the kitchen-door open, and a quantity of lucifer matches lying about—in my bed-room I found a plate-drawer had been opened, and missed the articles stated—the trowsers now produced were in the drawer of a bureau—I went with Lupton to a house in Lock's-fields, and there found a large bundle, containing these trowsers, breeches, and waistcoat, which are mine—I saw three children in bed there.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. I believe you prosecuted three parties, named Guilday, in Jan. last, for this robbery, and they were all convicted? A. Yes—I did not see the prisoner at the house in Lock's-fields.
HENRY LUPTON ( police-sergeant L 7.) On the night of the 24th of Dec. last I went to No. 9, York-street, Lock's-fields, from information I received—I did not find the prisoner there—three persons, named Guilday, were afterwards taken into custody—they were not there when I went—there were only four rooms in the house—I knocked at the street-door, an elderly woman opened it—I found these things in the front room up stairs, in the bed, covered over with the bed-clothes—three children were in the bed—the room-door was locked.
Cross-examined. Q. When you went to this house, did you find any of the Guildays there? A. The boy James Guilday, who was convicted in Jan.
last—I did not apprehend the others—they were taken about two hours and a half after—I first went into the room, on the ground floor—I found no property there—it was with the key of the back room ground floor that I opened the prisoner's room—I inquired for the key of the room, of the woman down stairs, and she said she knew nothing of it—the children were the prisoner's children—the bundle was in the bed, between the two sheets.
ELIZA TOOMEY . I am the wife of Daniel Toomey, and live at No. 9, York-street, Lock's-fields. The prisoner occupied the front room up stairs at the time of the robbery—he had three children—on Christmas eve I saw the prisoner go out at two o'clock in the day—I went out at eight, and did not return again till near twelve—the three Guildays were then in charge—they lived on the ground floor.
Cross-examined. Q. You have seen the prisoner in company with the Guildays in the house? A. Yes, talking to them, as I might do.
THOMAS TURVEY ( police-constable M 196.) I took the prisoner into custody in a public-house in the Borough—I told him he was wanted for the same case as the Guildays—he said, very well, he would go.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Mr. Common Sergeant.
[trialAccount t18430508-1691]
1691. LUCY SHAW was indicted for feloniously and knowingly uttering, on the 13th of March, a forged acquittance and receipt for 11 d., with intent to defraud John Smith.
JOHN SMITH. I am curator of the Royal Botanic Gardens at Kew. The prisoner was my servant—she had money to pay bills, and among the rest was this one of 11 d.—she told me she had paid it, and brought me this as a receipt, purporting to come from Mrs. Burbidge—I paid my bills on Tuesday—I afterwards found this out, went down stairs, and charged her with forging the name of Mrs. Burbidge—she made no denial—she put a paper into my hand, and said it would all be settled—I gave her in charge—she has put, "Paid, S. Burbidge."
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Did she not say she was very sorry, but intended to pay them? A. Yes.
SARAH BURBIDGE . I supply milk to Mr. Smith—on the 13th of March I had a bill against him of 1 s. 2 d. for milk—I have never received that—the signature to this bill is not mine, nor to this one of the 6th of March—I never received that money.
Cross-examined. Q. Did she tell you she had been in a great deal of trouble with her husband? A. Yes, and had kept back the money Mr. Smith had paid her to prevent his going to prison—she afterwards said that Mr. Smith's daughter would come and pay the last two weeks' bills, and that her brother was coming to settle every thing.
JAMES WILLIAM BURBIDGE . I am son of last witness—I never received this 11 d. from the prisoner or Mr. Smith—nobody else receives money for my mother.
MR. HORRY called
----. I am the prisoner's brother—her husband is a waiter, and he has been out of employment some time—her children have been ill, and two are dead—I offered to pay every farthing of this—she applied to me on the Sunday after she left the prosecutor, and before she was charged with this offence, and I promised to see into it on the Monday or Tuesday—I never knew a blemish on her character.
MR. SMITH re-examined. She was first charged with this on the 10th of April.
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430508-1692]
1692. LUCY SHAW was again indicted for knowingly uttering a forged receipt for 1 s. 2 d. on the 20th of March, with intent to defraud John Smith.
JOHN SMITH . I had weekly bills with Mrs. Burbidge for milk—on the 13th of March I owed her 1 s. 2 d.—the prisoner brought me this bill as receipted by Mrs. Burbidge.
SARAH BURBIDGE . This is not my writing—I never received the 1 s. 2 d.—before the prisoner was charged at all she came and told me that her brother was coming to settle every thing, and my bills should be paid.
Cross-examined. Q. Did she appoint to call on you on the Sunday? A. On the Saturday night—she did not come—she sent me word on the Monday that her brother was coming.
JAMES WILLIAM BURBIDGE . I have not received the 1 s. 2 d.—I know nothing of this writing.
FRANCES CREED . I found these other bills on the prisoner at the station.
NOT GUILTY .

[trialAccount t18430508-1694]
1694. THOMAS WHITE, THOMAS ALLISON, SARAH JONES , and SARAH FULLER , were indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of Elizabeth Hackett, about twelve in the night of the 9th of April, at St. John, Southwark, with intent to steal, and stealing therein, 24 shirts, value 2 l. 8 s., and 10 pairs of trowsers, 7 l., her property; and that White had been before convicted of felony.
ELIZABETH HACKETT . I am single, and keep a slop-shop in Thornton-street, Dockhead, in the parish of St. John. On Sunday night, the 9th of April, I went to bed at eleven o'clock—the house was all safe—just before twelve I heard a great noise, got up, opened the window, but could see nobody but a woman at the top of the alley—I went down about seven in the morning, found the fan-light over the door broken right in, sufficiently for a person to get into the house—a bundle of shirts was taken away, and ten pairs of trowsers, worth 9 l. 12 s. 6 d.
GEORGE GALE . I sell coffee and ginger-beer, and live in Brewer's-court, Horsleydown, in the next street to the prosecutrix—I saw the four prisoners that night, a little after four o'clock, with another not in custody, in Thornton-street,—they passed by me—they had nothing with them that I saw—I asked them if they wanted any coffee or ginger-beer—White made some remark and they went on about twenty yards, then stopped for about five minutes, and seemed to be considering—they came back—I had a cane on my truck—White took it up—I told him to put it down, it was not his property—we had a tustle to get it—then Allison came back to him, and said something—I thought I heard they should be blown or something, and he gave me the cane, they all five went on, and turned into New-street, which is at the back of Thornton-street—in ten minutes or a quarter of an hour I saw them come into an alley—White came forward, and seemed to be talking to somebody behind—they stood there some time, went back, and appeared to go into New-street, into Thornton-street—the female prisoners went and stood by the post-office, about 100 yards from the prosecutrix's—the male prisoners came up the street—Allison went down a court by the side of the house, and then White went down—I went towards the place, to see what they were doing—the male prisoners crossed the road, and went down towards the females, and the man not in custody walked down on the same side of the way as the prosecutrix's shop—that was the last I saw of them till I saw
the women in custody that evening, and White and Allison on the Saturday following.
Allison. I was not before the Magistrate. Witness. No, he was not—I never knew any of them before.
THOMAS TURVEY . I am a policeman. On Monday morning, the 10th of April, about nine o'clock, I was going on duty on Southwark-bridge-road—I saw all the prisoners and one not in custody in company—the two females had something in both their aprons—I asked them what they had—they said, "Some work"—I asked where they got it from—Fuller said her sister was in the habit of taking out work from a slop-shop, and they were going to take it home to their sister—I asked what they had to do to them, or if they were finished—they could not tell me—I asked them to let me look at them, which I did, and asked where their sister lived—they said a little way along the street, that is Norfolk-street—I said I would go with them—the men went on, and when they found I detained the women they all three ran away—I took the women to the station—in a few minutes after the prosecutrix came to give information of her shop being broken open and property stolen—she claimed the property—I had seen the three men frequently before—I am sure White and Allison are two of them.
White. I was forty yards from the females when you stopped them. Witness. He was not further than I am from him now—they were all five together—I had followed them nearly 150 yards—they were talking till they saw me close on them, and then the men made away from the women.
MRS. HACKETT re-examined. This is part of the property I lost—I know the shirts by the make, as well as by a particular mark in the cotton, which I noticed when I had them.
White's Defence. I met the two females on the other side of London-bridge; they asked me to give them something to drink; I said yes, if they came to the house I would. We went and had three or four pots of beer together; they asked me to give them a breakfast, which I did, at eight o'clock. I then said, "I must go to my work." I had got a little way along Southwark-bridge-road, and saw them coming after me. I afterwards looked behind, and saw them with the policemen I met two strange men in the street; this was not one.
Jones's Defence. On Sunday night a young woman who I knew asked me to mind the shirts for her; she must pawn them on Monday morning; she said she was taking them to her sister's; the policeman has told a great story.
Allison's Defence. I can prove where I was at the time the robbery was committed, but have not had time. I had nothing to do with the robbery.
WILLIAM KEATING . I am a policeman. I produce a certificate of White's former conviction, from the office of the Clerk of the Peace for Surrey—( read)—I was present at the trial—he is the man—he was tried by the name of John Smith.
WHITE — GUILTY . * Aged 22.— Transported for Life.
ALLISON — GUILTY .
JONES — GUILTY . Aged 20.
FULLER — GUILTY . Aged 17.
Transported for Fifteen Years.
[trialAccount t18430508-1695]
1695. DAVID BOYD was indicted for stealing, on the 6th of May, at St. John, Southwark, 10 watches, value 8 l.; 2 watch-cases, 1 l.; 1 timepiece, 12 s.; 1 coat, 15 s.; 1 handkerchief, 2 s. 6 d.; 10 seals, 4 l.; 10 watchkeys, 2 l. 10 s.; 3 split rings, 12 s.; 4 watch-chains, 4 s.; and 2 watch-guards, 4 s.; the goods of Samuel Nicholas, in his dwelling-house.
MARIA DANIELS . I am the wife of John Daniels, and lodge at the house of Mr. Nicholas. Last Sunday morning I awoke between six and seven o'clock,
and saw my door open—I then looked on the floor, and saw him—his head was under the bed—I jumped out of bed, took hold of the prisoner by the collar, and asked what he was doing there—he said he had been there all night—I gave an alarm, Mr. Nicholas came and took him till a policeman came—I went down into the yard, and found the back-door open—I found a bundle in a washing-tub there—I gave it to Mr. Nicholas.
SAMUEL NICHOLAS . I am a watch and clock maker, and live in Tooley-street, in the parish of St. John, Southwark. I fastened up my house at night, and went to bed a little before twelve o'clock—I was called in the morning, between six and seven, by Mrs. Daniels—I came down, and found the prisoner in her room, on his knees—I took charge of him, and sent for a policeman, who came, searched him in my presence, and produced several gold keys and gold seals, and various other things, which I knew to be say property—the bundle which Mrs. Daniels produced was opened in my presence, and contained a coat, ten watches, and other property—the value of all the property is 20 l. or more—the house was not broken into—I suppose he must have secreted himself in the shop over night—all the property now produced is mine, and was in my shop the night before.
DANIEL CARROLL . I am a policeman. These things have been in my care ever since.
Prisoner's Defence. I was walking down Tooley-street; I had no place to sleep in; I saw the door of this house open, and went in, having nowhere to go.
GUILTY . Aged 17.— Transported for Ten Years.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18430508-1696]
1696. WILLIAM WHEELWRIGHT and MICHAEL KIDNEY were indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of John Tilleard, about one in the night of the 12th of August, at St. George-the-Martyr, Southwark, with intent to steal.
JOHN HENRY TILLEARD . I am the son of John Tilleard, an oil and colourman, in Great Suffolk-street, St. George-the-Martyr, Southwark. On Tuesday night, the 11th of April, I closed the shop, and saw the premises safe—between one and two o'clock in the morning, I was aroused by the police—I found the sash of the door leading into the shop, forced—it had been safe overnight—there were marks on the door of some instrument having been used to force it.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Was it broken sufficient for a person to get in? A. Yes—the marks were on the middle door—the sash and shatter of the outer door were forced open.
FREDERICK LICHFIELD . I am a policeman. On the morning of the 12th of April, between one and two o'clock, I was in Great Suffolk-street—from information I received I hastened to the prosecutor's house—I saw both the prisoners there—Wheelwright had just come from the inside himself, and seeing me, he called out to somebody inside, "Look out, look out!"—I was within two paces of him—he ran across the street—I collared another, as he came out of the door, but he got away—Kidney followed that second man as he came out—he had no hat or cap on—the three attacked me, and prevented my sounding my rattle—they knocked it all to pieces, and sprained my hand—I knew them by sight before—I distinctly saw their faces—I saw the prisoners in custody next day, and knew them—I examined the door, and saw marks of a jemmy on it—I found this jemmy a few yards from the door—there were marks on the sash of the door, corresponding with this.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did the person you say was Kidney come out close to the one you held by the collar? A. He did—I cannot
say how I was knocked down—I was tripped up from behind—I gave a description of the men—I have not the least doubt of them—I should know the third man.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. You were a few paces from the door when Wheelwright appeared at it? A. About two paces—Wheelwright ran across the way, calling out to those inside, "Look out, look out!"—I never saw him again till he was in custody—Kidney came out within a minute or so after Wheelwright—he did not run away at the instant.
BENJAMIN PARSONS . I am a policeman. I took the two prisoners into custody on Thursday, the 13th of April, between three and four o'clock in the morning, at their lodging in Kent-street, both in the same house—they denied all knowledge of it.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did they deny all knowledge of the burglary? A. They did—they heard the charge at the station, and did not deny it in my presence—they denied it at their lodging, not at the station—I did not say, "From a description I have received I must take you, and mean to swear to you right or wrong"—Lichfield the constable, the inspector, and two or three more constables were present—I said nothing of the sort to either of them—tbe burglary was on the 11th—I took them on the 13th.
JAMES HARRISON ( police-constable M 195.) I know the prisoners by sight. On the night of the 11th of February, about a quarter past twelve, I saw them in Great Suffolk-street—Kidney was standing alone, about 40 yards from Wheelwright and Bevan, who is not in custody—about a quarter after one o'clock I saw Kidney standing in the doorway of a fishmonger's shop about forty yards from Mr. Tilleard's—I knew their persons, and was perfectly well acquainted with them.
Cross-examined by MR. PAYNE. Q. Did you speak to either of them? A. I did not—I crossed over from the opposite side, and passed them—when I saw Kidney in the fishmonger's door-way, I was on the same side of the way—he shammed drunkenness when he saw me, and reeled across the road—I had seen him not an hour before, and he could walk very well then.
Cross-examined by MR. HORRY. Q. Did you tell Parsons you had seen them? A. I did not, I did not see him till we went to Union-hall on the Thursday—I told Parsons then that they were the parties who I had seen loitering about, one had a hat on, and the other two caps.
COURT. Q. How was it they did not get any goods from the house? A. They had not time—I had seen my sergeant shortly before—I heard my brother constable call out "Stop thief"—I ran up, but they had escaped.
FREDERICK LITCHFIELD re-examined. This hat dropped off the man not in custody, in the struggle, and this cap was found in the shop—Kidney always wore a cap of this description, and he had a similar one on when apprehended, but perfectly new.
JURY. Q. What enabled you to see the parties so well? A. From the gas-light which was within a few yards of the door.
JAMES HARRISON re-examined. When I saw them before the robbery, one had a hat, and two had caps—Kidney had a cap of this description.
WHEELWRIGHT — GUILTY . Aged 21.
KIDNEY — GUILTY . Aged 20.
Transported for Ten Years.
[trialAccount t18430508-1697]
1697. WILLIAM HOLMES was indicted for bigamy.
JANE HEDGES . I am the wife of William Hedges, of Hampton; I have known the prisoner more than twenty years. In the autumn of 1829 I was present, at Teddington church, Middlesex, when he was married by banns to Maria Budd—she was about 22 or 23 years of age—I have seen her
to-day—I have known many marriages solemnized at Teddington church—Mr. Hoare was the clergyman officiating at the time—they lived together afterwards as man and wife for about nine months, very near me, at Hampton.
THOMAS WINCH ( police-sergeant N 40.) I produce an examined copy of the register of marriage between William Holmes and Maria Budd in Oct., 1829—I got it from the first wife—I went to the parish church at Teddington, and examined it with the register, in the clerk's room at his private residence, where the books are kept—they are not kept at the church—when I went about the certificate of the second marriage the book was fetched from the church to the clerk's house for me to examine it by—I had never seen the clerk before—I went to the church; the people opposite told me I should find the clerk at the house, and he showed me the book—there are several blanks in this certificate—they were so in the register.
JANE HEDGES re-examined. I knew the clergyman at the time—I know he was the officiating minister—he attended the church in his usual dress—it was before twelve o'clock.
WILLIAM GUNTON . I am a carpenter and joiner, and live at Kingston. I was occasionally employed as journeyman by the late William Turvey, who acted as parish clerk of Ham church—he was a master carpenter—I have known the prisoner eight or nine years—in 1840 I was at work at Turvey's house, and saw the prisoner and a woman named Harriet Talley, whom I knew before, go into Turvey's house; after remaining there some time they came out, and went with Turvey towards Ham parish church, which is about fifty yards from the house—I do not know whether they went into the church—they afterwards came back to the clerk's house, and went over to the Jolly Gardeners beer-shop.
JANE TALLEY . I live with my mother, Harriet Talley, in York-street, Rotherhithe. I have known the prisoner about twelve months—he and my mother lived as man and wife—they were so for aught I know—they used the same room.
THOMAS WINCH re-examined. I produce a paper purporting to be an examined copy of a marriage in Ham church—I got it from the clerk's house—he professed to be the clerk of Ham church—I do not know whether he was to—he showed me a book—I examined this copy with it—the clerk is since dead—he was one of the attesting witnesses to the marriage.
ELIZABETH NYE . I am a pew-opener at Ham parish church. Marriages are celebrated there, and have been so for many years—it was first a chapel, but is consecrated as a church—I have been pew-opener there nine years—I was present, in August, 1840, at a marriage between William Holmes and Harriet Talley—I cannot recognize the prisoner—there was some little difficulty in putting the ring on the bride's finger, but I did not see what it was—it was put on the left hand—the bride had on a dress of the clerk's daughter—the party went from the church to the clerk's house, and then to the Jolly Gardeners—I recollect hearing the banns put up three successive Sundays in the names of William Holmes and Harriet Talley—the Rev. James Hough is the clergyman who married them—the clerk is since dead—I did not know Talley before her marriage, and did not know her name, or whether she was single or a widow—she was described as a widow, and was asked in church by the name of Harriet Talley—I recollect that—the registers are usually kept in an iron chest in the vestry; but the clerk being very ill in bed, and not able to get up, and the clergyman being from home, they might be sent for to his house.
AMELIA SNOW . I am the wife of William Snow, and live in Lower Queen-street, Rotherhithe. I have known the prisoner eighteen years—he came to
live in the neighbourhood two years and a half ago, and introduced a person as his wife—I had not seen him for fifteen years before that—the woman wore a ring on her right hand—I asked why she did not wear it on her left—she said she could not, as she had two fingers joined together—I did not know her name before—I never knew her as Harriet Talley.
NOT GUILTY .
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.
