[trialAccount t18430508-1699]
1699. REBECCA RUMBALL was indicted for feloniously forcing, casting, and throwing Isaac Rumball into the water of a certain canal, with intent to kill and murder him.—2nd COUNT, stating it to be a male child of hers called Isaac.
RICHARD HEATH . I am a policeman. On Sunday, the 2nd of April, about ten o'clock in the evening, I saw the prisoner in Peckham-fields, north from Taylor's-bridge to Box-bridge—she was on the side footpath, crossing the fields, a very few yards from the canal—I said nothing to her then—I met her a second time, and said, "Good night, ma'am"—she said, "Good night, sir, good night"—she had a child at her breast—after she had passed me about five or ten minutes I heard some gentleman hallooing—I listened a moment, ran back, and when I got up the gentleman told me he thought there was somebody in the water—I turned my light on the water, and found a female there—I sang out for the drag, which was brought—I threw it in three times, and Ulrick got a boat and reached her—I and Taylor went over the other side of the canal, and pulled out the prisoner with a child lashed to her with this string—they both appeared to be dead—we took them into George Taylor's—a surgeon was sent for—they had recovered before the surgeon came—the string was tied round the child's neck, and two or three times round the prisoner's left arm.
Cross-examined by MR. CLARKSON. Q. When you said "Good night," she said "Good night, sir," did she say it in a hurried distracted manner? A. No—she was walking rather fast—she did not appear to answer me quickly—the child was a boy—it was a gentleman named Sparks who first gave the alarm—he is not here—she could have been but a short time in the water.
HENRY ULRICK . On the 2nd of April I heard an alarm given—I got a boat and took the prisoner out of the water with a child—there was a string round its neck and round the prisoner's waist and left wrist.
GEORGE TAYLOR . On the 2nd of April, I heard an alarm, and assisted in carrying the prisoner in—she is my sister-in-law—there was a child with her—it was her own—its name is Isaac Rumball—I am acquainted with the Humane Society—I used the proper methods to bring her and the child to life—they both recovered.
Cross-examined. Q. Did you know who she was when she was first brought out of the water? A. Not at first—it took about a quarter of an hour, or twenty minutes to recover her—the child was recovered in less than five minutes—from the position the child was found in, it would float above her—she is in the family way now—I did not know the father of the child—she has always been a kind affectionate creature—I never saw her otherwise—she is not married—she is 23, 24, or 25 years of age—her mother always kept the child—she is without any means of her own—she was in service at Stamford-hill—she was not going to leave—I was in the house when she threw herself into the water, and did not hear any splash.
COURT. Q. Where was she when you first saw her? A. By the side of the second bridge in the fields—I had helped my young man to take her out—I first saw her in the water.
THOMAS TRUNCHEON . On Sunday night, the 2nd of April, at a quarter to eleven o'clock, I found an umbrella and bag, containing two letters, about three yards from where the body was found, about three yards from the canal side—I delivered them to Day, the policeman—I saw them in Day's hands the next morning, and saw him give them to Taylor.
GEORGE TAYLOR re-examined. I have the umbrella and the bag, with the two letters in it—I believe they are the prisoner's handwriting; but can only swear to my belief—I have seen her write many times—the last time was three or four yean ago—I never saw her sign her name.
COURT. Q. Have you looked at the letters? A. Yes; there is some resemblance to her handwriting, but I cannot say.
ELIZA RUMBALL . I am the prisoner's sister—this is her bag—the letters are like her writing—I should not like to swear they are her's—I believe them to be so from the appearance—I have seen her write, and believe they are her's.
Cross-examined. Q. When did you last see her write? A. Since she has been in Newgate, some writing that came from her—I believe it was her writing—I have never seen much of her writing—I should not like to sweat that they are her's—I believe it to be her's—( read.)
"Mr. Taylor, Waterford Arms, Peckham: Ist April. Dear Ann and George,—I hope you will forgive me for all my past folly, and do the best you can with me with regard to bury me. I have a long time thought of this unhappy death. I see there is nothing but trouble for me, and happiness for others. Your unworthy sister— REBECCA RUMBALL."
"Stamford-hill. Dear mother and sisters,—I own I am very much indebted to you. I have brought on you an expense which is growing heavy on you. You were kind enough to say you would take him home; I have been thinking there will be much jealousy in your doing so, so I think of ending our lives in the canal, which I hope if George will not see us when alive, he will see us when dead, and hope you will bury us both together, me and my darling boy. I hope you and all my friends will pardon my sins. I am praying, and trying to seek repose in the bosom of God, which I have in vain sought for on earth; my own folly has brought me to this unhappy death. I hope you will think no more of me, but pray that my sins may be forgiven me, and that we may meet again in the next world. I hope you will forgive me. I hope you will all live happy together, my dear sister, you and George. Your dutiful daughter— REBECCA RUMBALL."
ELIZA RUMBALL Cross-examined. Q. Who is meant by George? A. My brother-in-law—he had not refused to set her—the prisoner was very fond of the child—it is three years old, and is very fond of her—the father is dead—she has not had much trouble—she did what she could to support the child with her wages.
GUILTY. Aged 24.— Strongly recommended to mercy. — Confined Two Years.
Before Edward Bullock, Esq.

[trialAccount t18430508-1702]
1702. JAMES COATES was indicted for stealing, on the 4th of Jan., 1 watch, value 6 l.; the goods of George William Deane and another, his masters.
MR. CLARKSON conducted the Prosecution.
GEORGE WILLIAM DEANE . I am in partnership with Thomas Winkley—we live in High-street, Borough, and are watchmakers. In Jan., 1842, I took the prisoner into our service in consequence of an advertisement, and fully approving of him, I raised his wages to 18 s. a-week—he boarded and lodged in the house exactly as one of my family—on Sunday evening, the 20th of Nov., he was taken ill, and on the Monday morning he was not able to get up—about a fortnight before that, or between a fortnight and three weeks, I had put into his hands a French gold hunting-watch to have the upper case raised, that the works might be free of the glass—the cases should have been taken off the works—when I gave it to him, I said, "I have determined on having the case raised so that it may free the glass, I have determined not to go to further expense," and I think I said, "It shall go to Fielder, in St. John-street-road," but whether I did or not, he knew that, because he was the only person I employed for that purpose—he was to have sent it by a lad I keep for that purpose, and I should have expected it would have been ready in two or three days, for the boy to have called again for it—it was not necessary that the works should have gone with the case—the prisoner knew that, decidedly—he was taken ill on the 20th of Nov., and a day or two afterwards I missed the movement of this watch—he was then very ill—I said nothing to him about it—he resumed his work on the 3rd of Jan., and on the following evening I brought him the whole of his work, which I had taken away from his work-board in the shop—I said to him, "Where is the French gold hunting watch, it is high time that was completed, and fit for stock?"—he said, "Bless me, sir, has not that French gold hunting-watch come home yet? why I sent it pretty nigh a month before I was taken ill; John took it"—I said, "Where to?"—he said, "To Jackson's"—I said, "Who is Jackson, is he a respectable man?"—he said, "O yes, a perfectly respectable man; the watch is safe enough"—I said, "Where does he live?"—he said, "At the back of Clerkenwell prison"—I said, "Well, but how is it I don't see the movement of the watch amongst this work which I have delivered to you; there was no occasion for the movement to be away?"—he said, "That is with it"—I said, "For what purpose is the movement with; the case?"—he said, "O, sir, the movement was foul of the dome, it would not close"—I said, "Well, I shall send John after it in the morning"—it was then about six in the evening—he slept in the house that night, and the next morning he got up to assist the lad in opening the shop—I was uneasy, and got up as soon as he did—I was down in the shop some time, and then I went to breakfast, expecting him to follow me, but he did not come—I found he was gone, and never returned—he had never assigned any reason to me for going, but he seemed to be better that morning—I took some pains to find Mr. Jackson, and inquired at the back of Clerkenwell prison—I found the prisoner on the evening of the 12th of Jan., at No. 4, Victoria-place, Kent-road—after some other conversation, I said, "Now, where is the French gold hunting-watch?"—after some hesitation, he said, "At Mr. Carter's"—I said, "Where
does he live?"—he said, "In President-street"—I gave him in charge—on the following evening I went to Mr. Carter's, I there found the gold hunting-watch—Mr. Carter also produced to me this pencil memorandum, which is the handwriting of the prisoner—( read)—"New cover and dome—Deane and Co., 169, Borough"—the dome is the inner case which coven the movement, and the cover is this outer case which I had given instructions to have raised—the effect of putting a new cover and a new dome on this watch, would be that I should not know the watch myself—it would take away the maker's name and the number, and destroy the identity of the watch altogether—I had never given him the slightest authority for any such alteration—it was altogether unnecessary.
Cross-examined by MR. DOANE. Q. It would make it like an English. watch? A. No—everybody knowing the nature of a watch would know was not English—the French name would be away—I first spoke to the prisoner on the 4th of January—he then said it was at Jackson's—he seemed very poorly, and I did not press him any further, intending to proceed the following day—he had the erisypelas in his head—Mr. Carter had repaired a watch at the end of December—I have at times given the prisoner returned watches to wear, which is a very common thing—he went away on the morning of the 5th of January, and his sister afterwards brought back a gold watch that he had with him when he went away—I am not a regular watchmaker myself, but I have had the handling of them for twenty-five years.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Have you been a housekeeper many years? A. Yes, and carried on business in this establishment, in which I was brought up.
JOHN MALYON . I am in the prosecutor's service—it is my business to carry out watches—I received this watch and another from the prisoner, to take to Mr. Fielder's, to inquire what it would come to to alter them—I took them, and brought them back, and told him Mr. Fielder said it would come to about 25 s.—he never gave me this watch back to take to Fielder—I do not know Mr. Jackson living behind the new prison at Clerkenwell—I never took anything there for the prisoner.
WILLIAM CARTER . I am a watch-case maker, and live in President-street, St. Luke's. I never saw the prisoner till he was in custody—this watch was brought to me, as I understand, about two hours after the prisoner was in custody—I think it was on the 12th of January—this written memorandum came with it—it was delivered at my street-door, to the servant girl, and brought up to me—I had no other direction than what this memorandum contains—Mr. Deane called on me the following day, and I produced to him the watch and the memorandum—I saw no necessity for a new dome, and I delayed doing it, and was going to Mr. Deane, though I did not know him, and have never, to my knowledge, done but one job for him—in order to raise the case to hold the glass, there was no necessity for a new dome—it would have destroyed the identity of the watch altogether—the movement is quite free of the dome.
WILLIAM LEVER ( police-constable P 119.) I took the prisoner on the 12th of January—he was discharged by the Magistrate—on the 1st of March I took him again on another charge—( memorandum read)—"New cover and dome. Deane and Co., 169, Borough."
NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430508-1703]
1703. JAMES COATES was again indicted for stealing, on the 3rd of January, 5 purse tassels, value 4 s.; three other purse tassels, 1 s.; 1 pair of purse slides, 6 d.; 5 shirt studs, 5 s.; 1 snap, 1 s. 6 d.; and 1 ear-ring, 1 s.; the goods of George William Deane and another, his masters.
GEORGE WILLIAM DEANE . In consequence of the absence of the prisoner, I caused a box of his that was left behind to be opened by the constable—it was locked—I found in it these five silver shirt studs, a black ear-ring top, and some purse slides—the whole I do not think exceeding 10 s. in value—I had never given the prisoner authority to take any portion of these articles—they are mine—some of them bear my own mark on them.
Prisoner. Q. What mark is on them? A. Here is a scratch, a private mark, which I believe you have no knowledge of—I had it before you were in my service—I can clearly see the marks on three out of five of these studs, and I have no doubt it is on the other two—this snap has my mark on it, and this black top—it is my custom to mark my goods—these silver studs are worth 1 s. each—I cannot say that I actually missed them till they were found—I then went to the drawer, where I knew they were—I had placed the purse tassels in the drawer a short time previous.
THOMAS WINKLEY . I am in partnership with Mr. Deane. I never gave the prisoner permission to take these things—I have nothing to do with this business.
WILLIAM LEVER ( police-constable 119 P.) I was called to examine the prisoner's box—it was locked—I opened it with a key which I took from the prisoner—I found these things in a small drawer in the box.
Prisoner. Q. Were they loose? A. Yes, I laid them on the beach before the Magistrate—he did not make any remark about them.
The prisoner called
CHARLOTTE COATES . I am the prisoner's sister. While he was in a state of unsound mind, Mrs. Deane had the key of his box—she kept it more than a week, because he was incapable of taking care of it—she said if he had the key he might take things out that would injure him—I saw her put the watch-guard, and rings, and several other things into the box.
MR. CLARKSON. Q. Were you living at Mrs. Deane's? A. No—I heard from her that she had the key of the box—when I wanted I used to ask her for it, and perhaps keep it all day—my brother has been in business for himself—he went by the name of James Wright, in consequence of a disagreement between him and his father.
Prisoner. These things were given by Mrs. Deane to the children to play with—I deny any knowledge of them.
GUILTY . Aged 26.— Confined One Year.
Before Mr. Recorder.
[trialAccount t18430508-1704]
1704. FREDERICK BROWN was indicted for stealing, on the 21st of April, 1 basket, value 9 d.; and 1 towel, 3 d., the goods of Edward Elliott; 1 loaf of bread, 3 d.; and a 1/4 lb. weight of pork, 2 d.; the goods of John Toff Steadman.
JOHN TOFF STEADMAN . I am in the service of Mr. Edward Elliott, and drive his van. On the 28th of April, I had a basket with a cloth in it, and some bread and meat, and cheese—I saw the prisoner and another come out of the Bald-faced Stag at Putney, as I was coming by—the prisoner came up to the near side of the van, and took the basket off a hook—I told him it was mine—he ran away, and threw it over the bridge—he was about twenty yards off when I overtook him.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. You had other parcels of considerable value? A. Yes—I had not known the prisoner before, nor the other man—I charged the other man, because he and the prisoner were together I thought—I saw the prisoner take the basket—I do not know whether he knew I saw him—I was feeding my horses when he took it off—he did
not run away—he stood and swore at me—I had been walking on the near side of my van, and the basket was on the near side.
COURT. Q. How far had he got from the cart when you first noticed him with the basket? A. About twenty yards—when he took it he put it under his arm—the other man did nothing—the bread and the meat could not be seen.
(The prisoner received a good character.)
GUILTY. Aged 24.— Recommended to mercy. — Confined Fourteen Days.

[trialAccount t18430508-1706]
1706. THOMAS ROGERS was indicted for uttering counterfeit coin.
MR. DOANE conducted the Prosecution.
THOMAS FREEMAN . I am a licensed victualler. On the 12th of March, the prisoner came to my place for half a pint of porter—I served him—he gave me a bad shilling—I gave him into custody—I marked the shilling—there were two other persons there—I kept it on the bar till I had served him, but it was in my sight all the time.
WILLIAM DRODGE . I am a policeman. On the 12th of March I took the prisoner, and received this shilling from Freeman—the prisoner was discharged, there being no other case against him.
JOHN HOLDER . I keep the Crown and Thistle, in South-Lambeth. On the 16th of March the prisoner came for some ale—he gave me a half-crown—I gave him 2 s. 4 1/2 d. change—I put the half-crown into the till—there was no other there—my wife soon called my attention to it, and I found it was bad—on the 30th of May the prisoner came again, and gave my wife a half-crown—I received it from her—I sent for an officer, and gave him in charge.
RHODA HOLDER . I am the wife of John Holder. On the 16th of March the prisoner called for a glass of ale—I after that called my husband's attention to the half-crown I found in the till—on the 30th the prisoner called again for half a pint of porter, and gave me a bad half-crown—I gave it my husband.
JOHN MAURICE ( police-constable V 70.) I took the prisoner on the 30th and received these two half-crowns from Mr. Holder—I only found a half-penny on the prisoner.
MR. JOHN FIELD . I am inspector of coin to the Mint. These two half-crowns are counterfeit, and this shilling is also counterfeit.
GUILTY . Aged 20.— Confined Six Months.
[trialAccount t18430508-1707]
1707. JAMES PALLETT was indicted for bigamy, to which he pleaded

[trialAccount t18430508-1708]
1708. JOHN HERNE was indicted for burglariously breaking and entering the dwelling-house of John Bennett, about the hour of two in the night of the 12th of April, at Lambeth, with intent to steal, and stealing therein, 1 coat, value 10 s.; 1 kettle, 6 s.; 1 cloak, 2 s.; 1 weighing-machine, 10 s.; 3 pairs of shoes, 21 s.; 1 candlestick, 2 s.; 3lbs. weight of pork, 15 d.; and 6lbs. weight of ham, 4 s.; the goods of John Bennett; and PHILIP ROWLAND for feloniously receiving the same, well-knowing it to have been stolen; against the Statute, &c.
MR. RYLAND conducted the Prosecution.
JOHN BENNETT . I am a cow keeper, residing in North Brixton, in the
parish of St. Mary, Lambeth. On the evening of Wednesday, the 12th of April, I secured my house myself—the kitchen window fastens with a button inside—there is a dairy within my premises—I fastened the dairy-door up myself—I went to bed about eleven, leaving everything safe—between two and three in the morning I thought I heard a bell ring—I got up, knocked against the door, and then went to bed again, hearing no answer—I got up as usual, about five, and went out to milk some cows—I did not examine my house—I got back about a quarter to six, and went to the dairy, which I had fastened the over night, and missed a side of pork, a sheep's tongue, and half a ham from there—from the kitchen I missed a cloak, a coat, a pair of scales, three pairs of shoes, a copper kettle, and a candlestick—I examined the kitchen window, and found it had been cut through by some instrument from outside, and cut the beading of the stop so as to come to the button—they had turned the button round, and a person had got in there—there was the print of the heels—the dairy-door was cut so that something could be put in, and push back the bolt—I was the first person that came down that morning, and the last that went to bed the night before—I have seen some of the property since—it is mine—it was safe in the house over night—it is worth 3 l. or 4 l.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Was the prisoner the policeman in your neighbourhood? A. No.
RICHARD LAMBURN ( police-constable D 128.) On Thursday morning, the 13th of April, the day before Good Friday, between nine and ten, I was in bed—I had been out on night duty—my wife called me up to see Herne—he was in the police at that time—he said he knew of a cheap bargain of some things to be sold belonging to a poor man, and wanted me to go down and look at them—he said he thought I could have them cheap, as the poor man was hard up —he wanted 23 s. for them, and he should not be surprised if I could have them for less—I was to go to No. 54, James-street, which is about half a mile from Mr. Bennett's—I had heard that Rowland lived there—I went to No. 54, James-street, and there found a Petersham coat and cloak, some scales, and two pairs of shoes—they have since been claimed by the prosecutor—I left 23 s. with Herne in case he could not get them for less-on Easter Monday he came down to my house with a boy, and brought the things—he told me he was going to take the boy to show him the races—he said he was obliged to give the 23 s., and he left the things with me—they continued at my house till I gave them up to the officer—on the Wednesday Herne came again and told me he had been buying a candlestick cheap, he gave 6 d. for it, and thought it was worth the money as old brass—he brought it down a day or two after—I told him I was going to take an iron boiler to Baalzephon-street, Bermondsey, and we took the copper kettle with it—we mentioned the brass candlestick, and a person who lived in Mrs. Edwards's house, said she wanted one, she came over to me the following Wednesday, and purchased it—I had given in my resignation, and was about going into business myself to buy and sell.
Cross-examined. Q. Are you in the police now? A. Yes—I have not been on duty since—I have not heard that I am suspended—I have been on this case—I did not hear anything of this till the 1st of May, and then I gave the things up to Sergeant Slack—I swear I was dealing with these articles, and other articles also—I had nine waistcoats, and some handkerchiefs, which I bought at Burlton's, a pawnbroker in the Walworth-road—I am going to set up a shop, to sell second-hand clothes—I was selling all sorts of articles—some of these things were sent down into the country—Herne and I were going into business together—I thought he was a straight-forward man, and we
were going into the country with a horse and cart—I thought him an honest man—I believe my superior officers have got nothing against me—I do not know whether they know that I am dealing in articles of this description—it is contrary to our regulations to do this—I believe I first began to deal in these articles on the day I sent in my resignation—I had dealt in articles for my own family before that time—I sent in my resignation a month last Wednesday—I cannot recollect how long it was before Good Friday.
Q. What other things did you take to Mrs. Edwards, besides these? A. An iron boiler, a pair of trowsers and a waistcoat, to see if she would buy them—I bought the iron boiler at Birmingham, ten years ago—the waistcoats I bought at Burlton's, the pawnbroker's, a few days before, and the trowsers in the New-cut—Mrs. Edwards is a fellmonger—on the 13th Herne was on duty somewhere in the Brixton-road—I do not do duty there myself—I gave information directly I suspected the things were stolen—I inquired of my brother officer what Herne was locked up for—he said it was for something at North Brixton, and gave me a description of a few things which were stolen—I have been in the tailoring business before I came to London, at Thornbury, in Buckinghamshire.
ELIZABETH LAMBURN . On Thursday morning, the 13th of April, Herne came to my house, and asked if my husband was at home—I said, "Yes, in bed"—he said he wanted to see him, he had seen some things which he thought he could buy cheap, and he wished him to go and see them, they belonged to a poor man who was hard up—I called my husband twice, and he came down—Herne repeated the same words to him that he told me—they went out together—on Easter Monday he came again, and brought three pairs of shoes, a Petersham coat and cloak, and a copper tea-kettle, and left them—they remained there till Slack came, and my husband delivered them up, as soon as he had suspicions they were stolen.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Have you ever been in the way of dealing with various articles? A. Never—I have been married a long time—I live in James-street, Camberwell—not the same James-street that Herne lived in—we have the house to ourselves—I did not think it an extraordinary thing that a policeman should have such property as this.
MARY EDWARDS . I am the wife of Henry Edwards, and live in Baalzephon-street, Long-lane, Bermondsey. I have seen Herne once—Lamburn and he came to my house, and brought an iron boiler and a tea-kettle—Lamburn asked me to buy the kettle—I declined—he said he had got a candlestick—Herne was by at the time—I had bought the boiler of Mrs. Lamburn some time before—Hutchings lodged at my house at the time.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST? A. Did you buy any waistcoats? A. We had spoken to Lamburn about them—Herne came apparently to carry them—he did not appear to be bargaining or taking any interest in them—he asked what I thought of their going trafficking—he said to my mother, "What do you think of our concern?"—I had known Lamburn some time—we had lived together two years in one house, at Peckham—I had heard from Lamburn's wife, on Easter Monday, that Lamburn proposed that Herne and he should go about—I had heard Lamburn was going to leave the police.
JANE HUTCHINGS . I was lodging with Mrs. Edwards—I bought this brass candlestick of Lamburn for 1 s., at his house at Peckham.
Cross-examined. Q. Had he many articles for sale? A. Yes, he showed me various articles, a coat and cloak, a copper tea-kettle, scales, and waistcoats.
THOMAS SLACK ( police-constable P 3.) I produce some shoes and other
articles which I got at Lamburn's house—he was No. 128 in the same division as the prisoner.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Are you the superintendent over Herne? A. No, sergeant—he was not on duty in Addison-place North, Brixton-road, but on the next beat, but it nearly came to the place where the prosecutor lives—Smith did duty in that street—he is discharged—Herne lived at No. 39, James-street, and Rowland lived at No. 54, James-street.
MR. RYLAND. Q. Was Smith discharged in consequence of this burglary? A. Yes—Herne had the night duty, on Wednesday night, on the adjoining beat.
SAMPSON DARKIN CAMPBELL . I am an inspector of the P division. In consequence of information, on Sunday, the 30th of April, I apprehended Herne at the corner of James-street—on Tuesday, the 2nd of May, I went to No. 54, James-street, with Kendall—I had heard Rowland lived there-he was not at home—I had some conversation with his wife—he afterwards came in—I told him I had come to his house to make some inquiries relative to some property that had been left at his house by Herne the policeman-I read the list of them from this paper—( reads)—"One brown Petershars great coat, one brown cloak, one copper tea-kettle, one brass candlestick, a black velvet waistcoat, a copper scale, with dial, one side of pork, and three pairs of shoes"—he said, "These things were never brought here"—his wife was present, so as to hear that—I said, "You had better be on your guard, your wife has already admitted that the things were brought here"—she said, "You had better tell the truth"—he then said, "On the morning of the 13th of April, between two and three o'clock, I was in the road, near Mr. Myatt's garden, when Herne and Smith came up to me; Herne gave me a side of pork and half a ham, which I carried home; shortly after, while I was lacing my boots, Herne came in with the other things"—Kendall asked him how he was positive as to the day—he said because it was the day before Good Friday, market morning, and that was the reason he was out so early.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. Did you find any of these articles at his house? A. No—I had previously communicated to his wife what I came for—I had got information from Lamburn, who was in our division—he is not discharged—he has been reported to the Commissioners—Herne was acting as a policeman at the time the robbery took place—Rowland's wife was very much alarmed—Rowland did not exhibit any agitation; and when Kendall asked him about the day, he gave every information—he seemed to fix my attention to that day, that I might make no mistake.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. This conversation with Rowland was relative to the things which were said to have been brought by Lamburn? A. Yes—Rowland admitted that they were at his house—it was at Rowland's house that I had the conversation—Herne lives at No. 54, nearly opposite—his wife was not living with him at that time—the police are not allowed to trade at all, or to buy any articles but for their own use—as they had given notice to quit, I should not have reported about their buying a stock of goods, but in ordinary cases I should.
Cross-examined by MR. BALLANTINE. Q. You have been a long time acquainted with the neighbourhood where Rowland lives, and knew him? A. I have known him as a servant of Mr. Myatt—I never knew any thing against him before.
Cross-examined by MR. PRENDERGAST. Q. Nor against Herne? A. Not as to his honesty—Myatt is a large market-gardener—Rowland was his carter.
PETER KENDALL ( police-sergeant P 1.) I went with Campbell, on the 2nd of May, to Rowland's house—I examined Mr. Bennett's premises last Sunday—the outside frame of the kitchen window had been cut, apparently with a knife, so as to admit an instrument to turn the button inside; and that being undone, the opening was large enough for a man to get in—I examined the dairy door, and a portion of the wood was cut where the bolt of the lock shuts, so as to admit an instrument to put the bolt back—I got this candlestick from Mr. Hutchings.
JOHN BENNETT re-examined. Q. Did the kitchen window and dairy door remain in the state in which they were, on the morning after the robbery, till last Sunday? A. Yes.
SAMSON DARKIN CAMPBELL re-examined. I was present when the prisoners were examined before Mr. Trail—they were asked if they wished to say any thing, and cautioned in the usual way—what they said was taken down in writing—this is Mr. Trail's signature—( read)—"The prisoner Herne declines saying any thing at present. The prisoner Rowland says, 'On the Thursday before Good Friday Herne bought the things, and on the Thursday I had the meat in the lane from Herne.'"
HERNE — GUILTY . Aged 27.— Transported for Life.
ROWLAND — NOT GUILTY .
[trialAccount t18430508-1709]
1709. CHARLES COOK was indicted for stealing, on the 11th of May, 1 spoon, value 12 s., the goods of Richard Elworthy, his master.
RICHARD ELWORTHY . I am a cowkeeper, and live at Bermondsey. The prisoner was in my service. I missed a spoon about one o'clock on Thursday last—I had not seen it since the Monday—this it it—I have the fellow to it in my pocket.
GEORGE POOLE . I am a pawnbroker, and live in New Park-street. The prisoner came to my shop, and offered to pawn this spoon, on Thursday last—he asked 5 s. for it—he said it was his own, and it appeared as if it had just been used—I stopped it—he had a horse and cart with him—the prosecutor's name was on the cart.
Prisoner. I did not take it to the shop. Witness. I am certain it was him—he said he gave 6 s. 6 d. to a pawnbroker for it.
GUILTY .— Confined Three Months.
ADJOURNED TO MONDAY, JUNE 12 TH, 1843.
